Code CFTs and Topological Matter
E.H Saidi, R. Sammani

Abstract
In this paper, we propose a novel framework for modeling topological phases of matter using code-based Narain conformal field theories (NCFTs). We show that the algebraic structure of the NCFTs naturally embeds into critical lattice quantum field theory, yielding emergent topological features characteristic of gapless fermionic systems with non-zero Chern numbers. We develop a new representation of construction A of code CFT in terms of root and weight lattices of Lie algebras, focusing in particular on SU(2) and SU(3). We then derive the spectrum of particle states occupying the lattice and, with the help of fermionisation techniques, demonstrate that it hosts fermionic excitations with Dirac cones akin to tight binding systems namely the Haldane model of quantum anomalous hall effect on a honeycomb geometry.



1
Introduction

Condensed matter systems, especially in their lattice QFT
formulations, have long provided a testing ground for concepts originating
in high energy physics theory
R1
;
R2
. These models allow the
realizability of theoretical frameworks that are otherwise difficult or even
impossible to test. Beyond the experimental validation, they also offer
insights on testable predictions that may open pathways towards the
development of potential technologies
R3
.

In this work, we introduce a novel approach to modeling topological phases
of matter by proposing code-based conformal field theories (CFTs)
1
-
1hA
as a new class of realizations. Exploiting recent constructions
of Narain CFT as a coding theory
1hA
-
1cB
where one can
systematically build and analyze Narain CFTs using self-dual additive codes
defined over discrete abelian groups, we demonstrate that the associated
algebraic data can be leveraged to model topologically non trivial phases of
matter
R4
-
trs2
. We show that the framework of code based
Narain CFT is not only mathematically rich but also physically relevant with
emergent topological properties
AZ
-
DSL
characteristic of a
gapless fermionic system having a non zero Chern number
R6
;
R7
. In
doing so, we provide a new framework for describing topological matter as a
code based CFT.

For every even self dual code
\mathcal{C}
, one can construct a triplet of
lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}\subset\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}\subset(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}})^{\ast}
realizing
the Narain CFT with U(1)
{}^{\mathrm{c}_{\text{{l}}}}\times
U(1)
{}^{\mathrm{c}_{\text{{r}}}}
where
\mathrm{c}_{\text{{l}}}=\mathrm{c}_{\text{{r}}}=\mathrm{r}

1
-
1kA
that
admits a holographic dual given by the AB Chern-Simons (CS) theory with
coupling constant
\mathrm{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
(CS level)
1B
; report to
appendix A
for a comprehensive review. These
constructions allow to perform explicit calculations of generalised Narain
partition functions using code based algorithms
1cB
. The
structure of the even self dual codes
\mathcal{C}
enables to build
discrete sets
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}
sitting in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}
which in turn realise 2r dimensional Narain lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\text{{narain}}}
. In this setting, the discrete group
G_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}
is identified with the discriminant
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}\ast}/\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}
taken in this study to be
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r}}
and can be
interpreted in terms of the symmetries of unit cells of the real lattice
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}},
its dual
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\ast\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}
and the even self dual
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{r},\mathrm{r}}.
This
construction, referred to as Construction A
1jB
, permits to generate
KK and winding states, related via duality, and populating a lattice (a
honeycomb for SU(3) with k=2) that hosts fermionic hoppings. The
resulting lattice model is shown to mimic that of Haldane theory with Dirac
cones.

Our investigation unfolds through the following steps:

(1)

In these expressions, the root lattice
R
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{2}}
is isomorphic to the 1D lattice
\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}\mathbb{Z}
while the weight lattice
W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{2}}

distant by some

these lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}
describe quantum particle states
\left|{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 288$}}}_{a,n}^{b,m}\right\rangle
labeled by four indices: ground
configuration indices (a,b), Kaluza

and windings
m
modes.

(\mathbf{b})

The SU(3) extension
: We construct novel
realisations of construction A of code CFT, extending beyond the SU(2) based
configurations
HS1
-
HS4
discussed previously. In this
extension, we build upon the SU(2) results to generalise the lattice
realisation (
A.147
) to higher dimensional Lie algebras especially those
of rank 2 such as SU(3), SO(5) and G2. Focussing on the interesting case of
SU(3), the relevant 4D lattices are embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{2},\mathrm{2}}
with hierarchy
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\subset\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\subset\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\ast\mathbf{su}_{3}}
and
representation:

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}

=

\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}

=

\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times
W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\ast\mathbf{su}_{3}}

=

W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times
W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}

(2)

We distinguish three cases according to the value of the Chern-Simons level
k of the holographic dual of the Narain CFT:
\left(\mathbf{i}\right)

Case k=3
: A this level, the discriminant group is
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}
with
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}
being the group centre of SU(3). The 4D lattice
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}=
R
{}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times
R
{}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
is made out of
the fibration of two hexagonal lattices while its dual
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\ast\mathbf{su}_{3}}=
W
{}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times
W
{}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
is constructed from the fibration of two triangular lattices.
\left(\mathbf{ii}\right)

Case k
>
3
: the results obtained for k=3
extends naturally to higher values k
>
3 for which the
discriminant satisfies
W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}/
R
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{k}}.

\left(\mathbf{iii}\right)

Case k
<
3
: for low levels
\mathrm{k}=1,2
, the situation is subtler. While the CS level k=1 is somehow
”exotic” because the condition
R
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\subseteq
W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
, as we
will discuss, is violated. This bound holds only for k
\geq\sqrt{3}

therefore ruling out k=1 but allowing k=2 for which the weight lattice

W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
is given by the 2D honeycomb
while the
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
is an hexagonal
lattice.

(\mathbf{c})

Emergence of topological properties
: After
establishing a new formulation of the framework in terms of algebraic data,
we exploit the new setting to develop a path towards topological phases of
matter by linking Narain CFT to critical lattice QFT. We start by modeling
the particles sitting at the intersection of the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
grid in terms of KK
\left|KK_{\mathbf{m}}\right\rangle
and
winding states
\left|W_{\mathbf{m}}\right\rangle
akin to
tight-binding systems in condensed matter physics. Our primary focus remains
on SU(2) based code CFTs with central charge giving quantized left/right
momenta for particle states, organizing into SU(2) representations, and
labeled by ground state and excitation quantum numbers. This reveals a
Hilbert space containing k
2
vacuum states and associated excitations, a
decoupling of energy and momentum sectors, in addition to a globally defined
topological index depending on k.

Proceeding, we analyze the particle content in the SU(3) Chern-Simons (CS)
theory at level k=2. We show that the corresponding weight lattice
decomposes into two sublattices forming a honeycomb structure,
interconnected via a Z
2
symmetry. This later also relates the
KK modes residing on one sublattice to the winding modes populating the
other. Using fermionisation techniques
ferm1
;
ferm2
;
T
, we
introduce Dirac fermions as propagations on the honeycomb lattice with a
tight binding Hamiltonian that breaks inversion symmetry. We prove that our
lattice model mimics that of Haldane theory
tm
;
tm1
with Dirac cones
therefore demonstrating that NCFTs can be realised as gapless fermionic
systems having non zero Chern numbers. This in turn closes the loop and
establishes a direct link between code CFT and topological matter.

The organisation is as follows: In
section 2
, we present construction
A in code-CFTs realising Narain conformal theories and show how the three
lattices
\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\ast}\right)
can be linked
to the root
R
{}^{\mathbf{su}_{2}}
and the weight
W
{}^{\mathbf{su}_{2}}
lattices of SU(2) and their cross products. New results
for this SU(2) based building are obtained. In
section 3
, we extend
the construction to SU(3) and derive realisations of the triplet
(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\ast})
while distinguishing three intervals k=3, k
>
3 and k
<
3. In
section 4
, we give applications aiming to
establish a link between the Narain CFTs and lattice quantum matter at Dirac
points. Here, we investigate the properties of particle states in the SU(2)
and the SU(3) based CFTs while in
section 5
we draw the way to Chern
matter by giving explicit examples.
section 6
is devoted to conclusion
and discussions.
Appendix A
,
Appendix B
and
Appendix C

contain technical details of our calculations and supplementary material
supporting the main text.

The triplet (
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\ast}
) from SU(2)

in higher dimensions.

\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{k}}/\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{k}}

This coset space will later be given a compelling geometric interpretation.
By viewing

\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{k}}\simeq\underbrace{\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}\text{ }\cup\text{ {R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{[\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}]}\text{ }\cup\text{ }...\text{ }\cup\text{ {R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{[(\mathrm{k-1)}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}]}}_{\mathrm{k}\text{ components}}\text{ }\subset\text{ }\mathbb{R}

The structure of the lattice

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}

Physically, red sites will be thought of as filled by KK particle states and

The set of red sites represents the sublattice

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}/\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}
which, up to a global shift
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}},}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}
. Hence, we have the
decomposition

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}

=

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}+\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}/\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}\right)

\simeq

\underbrace{\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}+\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}+\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}\right)}_{2\text{ superposed }\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}\text{s }}

(9)

To derive the results illustrated in Figures
2
-
3
, we start with eq(
B.9
) which shows that the lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast},

realised in terms of
R
2
and
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}},

have the following factorised structure

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}=\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}\times\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\prime}\qquad,\qquad\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}=\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}\times\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\prime}

(10)

Since
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}/
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{2}},
the discriminant
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}/\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}
is isomorphic to the discrete group
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{2}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{2}}
. To elucidate the SU(2)
interpretation of the lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}},

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}},

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast},

we reconsider the discrete sets
\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}}\mathbb{Z}
as follows:

\left(\mathbf{1}\right)

sublattice

\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}
.

The presence of
\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}
viewed as
\sqrt{1^{2}+1^{2}}
, suggest that the discrete set
\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}

correspond to a 1D sublattice of the ambient square lattice
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
with canonical basis vectors (
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}
) and lattice spacing
a
=\mathtt{b}=1
. This
sublattice can be realised in two equivalent ways:

\left(\mathbf{i}\right)
As the
diagonal 1D sublattice
with
coordinates (
n,n
) where
n\in\mathbb{Z};
see red dots in Figure
4
.

\left(\mathbf{ii}\right)
As the
anti-diagonal sublattice
with
coordinates (
n,-n
); see the blue dots in the same Figure
4
. These (anti) diagonal lines in
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
have slopes
\frac{b}{a}=\pm 1
and spacing
\mathtt{c}=\sqrt{\mathtt{a}^{2}+\mathtt{b}^{2}}=\sqrt{2}
.

Figure 4:
On top, the square lattice
\mathbb{Z\times Z}
. (anti-) diagonal
1D sublattice
\pm\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}
is given the (blue)
red sites. In bottom, the anti-diagonal 1D sublattice to be used below.

For our purposes, we adopt the anti-diagonal realisation of
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\sim\sqrt{2}\mathbb{Z}
with sites (
n,-n
). This sublattice is
generated by the vector
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}=\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{2}

with components
(1,-1)
satisfying
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}^{2}=2
and norm
\left\|\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}\right\|=\sqrt{2}.
We can therefore define
the 1D sublattice like

\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}

(11)

By using the unit vector
\mathbf{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{2}\right)
in the direction of
R
2
, we
may also write
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}=\sqrt{2}\mathbf{u}
and then
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq(\sqrt{2}\mathbb{Z})\mathbf{u}.

\left(\mathbf{2}\right)

sublattice

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}}\mathbb{Z}
.

This lattice, denoted
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}}\mathbb{Z}
, is dual to
\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}.
It is generated by
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}
with components

(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2})
, euclidian length
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}^{2}=1/2

and norm
\left\|\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}\right\|=1/\sqrt{\mathrm{2}};

precisely the inverses of the corresponding root quantities
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}^{2}
and
\left\|\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}\right\|.
The lattice
W
2
sits along
the anti-diagonal line
of the square
lattice
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\times\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
with lattice
spacing
\mathtt{\tilde{a}}=\frac{1}{2}
leading to
\mathtt{\tilde{c}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}.
Thus

\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}

(12)

Again, in terms of the unit vector
\mathbf{u},
we also find
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{u}
implying
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbb{Z})\mathbf{u}.
For illustration, see the
Figure
5
.

Figure 5:
On top, we give the
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z\times}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and anti-diagonal 1D with blue and red sites. In bottom, we find the
anti-diagonal 1D lattice
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}}\mathbb{Z}

containing
\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}
as a sublattice.

In this parametrisation of
\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}}\mathbb{Z}
, the generators
\textstyle\mathchar 267
and
\textstyle\mathchar 277
are very remarkable in the sense they are familiar in Lie algebra
theory. When endowed with the euclidian metric
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 270$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 270$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 270$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 270$}}}_{ij}
,
\textstyle\mathchar 267
and
\textstyle\mathchar 277
correspond to the simple root and the
fundamental weight vectors of SU(2) with
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}^{2}=2
and
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}^{2}=1/2
and obeying the duality relation
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}=1
. Therefore, we can accordingly characterise the lattices

R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}\mathbb{Z}
and
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{2}}}\mathbb{Z}
as follows:

\displaystyle\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{x}_{n}=n\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}\text{ \mbox{$|$} }n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\},\qquad\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{n}}\right\rangle=2n^{2}

(13)

\displaystyle\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{k}_{m}=m\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}\text{ \mbox{$|$} }m\in\mathbb{Z}\right\},\qquad\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{k}_{m}\right\rangle=nm

(14)

From the relation
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}},}

the dual weight lattice
W
2
splits into two subsets

W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small even}}
and
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small odd}}
as described below:

\left(\mathbf{i}\right)

the
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small even}}
, generated by
even
integers
m=2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}
, gives vectors
\mathbf{k}_{2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}}=2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
which are isomorphic to root vectors
\textstyle\mathchar 278
\textstyle\mathchar 267
; as such, we have

{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small even}}\simeq

. This corresponds to the blue lD

.

\left(\mathbf{ii}\right)

the subset

{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small odd}},
made of
odd
vectors
\mathbf{k}_{2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}-1}=\left(2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}+1\right)\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
that read also like
\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}},}
describes root vectors
\textstyle\mathchar 278
\textstyle\mathchar 267
shifted by a fundamental weight
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}
. Thus

{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small odd}}
is

{}_{\mathrm{2}}+\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
; it corresponds to
the red lD lattice in Figure
5
. Hence,
W
2
decomposes into the superposition

\begin{tabular}[]{lllll}$\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}$&$=$&$\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small even}}$&$\cup$&$\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small odd}}$\\
&$=$&$\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}$&$\cup$&$\left\{\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}+\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}\right\}$\end{tabular}\qquad,\qquad\begin{tabular}[]{lll}$\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small even}}$&$\simeq$&$\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}$\\
$\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{2}}^{{\small odd}}$&$\simeq$&$\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{2}}+\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}$\end{tabular}

(15)

in agreement with the discriminant
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}/
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{2}}
.

2.1.2
Lattices of SO(4)

Here, we use equation (
10
) to construct the two dimensional lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}},}

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}

and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}
along with their respective
characteristic matrices represented by the same symbols
\Lambda,

\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\Lambda^{\ast}.

A.

the triplet (
\mathbf{\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}},\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{,\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}
)
and

SO(4) lattices

Using the above results, the 2D lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}\simeq
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\times
R
2
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}\simeq
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\times
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}},
given by eq(
10
), can be naturally
interpreted as the 2D root
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\mathbf{so}_{\mathbf{4}}}
and the 2D weight
W
{}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\mathbf{so}_{\mathbf{4}}}

lattices of
SU(2)\times SU(2)^{\prime}
. This group is isomorphic to
SO(4), which possesses two orthogonal simple roots (
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}^{\prime}
) and two orthogonal fundamental weight vectors (
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}\mathbf{,{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}^{\prime}
). Within this view, the site vectors
\mathbf{v}_{n}
and
\mathbf{w}_{m}
in the lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}

and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}
are therefore parameterised by
pairs of integers (
n\mathbf{,}n^{\prime}
) and (
m\mathbf{,}m^{\prime}
)
as follows

\begin{tabular}[]{lll}$\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}$&$=$&$\left\{\mathbf{v}_{n}=n\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}\oplus n^{\prime}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}^{\prime}\right\}$\\
$\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}$&$=$&$\left\{\mathbf{w}_{m}=m\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}\oplus m^{\prime}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}^{\prime}\right\}$\end{tabular},\qquad\begin{tabular}[]{lll}$\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}$&$=$&$\{\mathbf{v}_{n}=\sqrt{2}\left(n^{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}+n^{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}\right)\}$\\
$\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}$&$=$&$\{\mathbf{w}_{m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(m^{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}+m^{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}\right)\}$\end{tabular}

(16)

where
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}=\sqrt{2}\mathbf{u}_{1}
,
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}\mathbf{u}_{2}
and
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{u}_{1},

\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{u}_{2}
. They
are represented in Figure
2
. Notice that in terms of the
components
\mathbf{u}_{1}=(1,0)
and
\mathbf{u}_{2}=(0,1)
with Lorentzian
product
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{T}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}\mathbf{u}_{j}={{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}_{ij},
we have

v_{n}^{i}=\sqrt{2}u_{j}^{i}n^{j}\qquad,\qquad w_{m}^{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}u_{j}^{i}m^{j}

(17)

The characteristic matrices follow as
\left(\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}\right)_{j}^{i}=\sqrt{2}u_{j}^{i}
and
\left(\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}\right)_{j}^{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}u_{j}^{i}.
From the relationships (
16
), we
deduce the realisation of
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}\simeq
R
{}_{\mathrm{2}}\times
W
2
reading like

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}=\{\mathbf{u}_{n,m}=n\sqrt{2}\mathbf{u}_{1}+\frac{m}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbf{u}_{2}\}

(18)

The lattice picture is depicted by the Figure
3
.

B.

characteristic matrices

Using the components of the vectors
\mathbf{v}_{n}
and
\mathbf{w}_{m}

given by (
17
), the characteristic matrices of the real and dual
lattices are respectively given by
\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}=\sqrt{2}I_{2\times 2}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}I_{2\times 2}.
To
express the matrix
\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}},
it is convenient to
decompose the identity matrix
I_{2\times 2}
in terms of the projectors
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{1}
and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{2}
defined as follows

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)\qquad,\qquad{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

(19)

In term of these
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{i}
’s, the characteristic matrices of the
triplet
(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast})
can be written as

\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}=\sqrt{2}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{1}+\sqrt{2}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{2},\qquad\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{2},\qquad\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}}=\sqrt{2}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 293$}}}_{2}

(20)

2.2
Models with CS level

k
>
2

For higher values of the integer k, while keeping the central charge
c_{\text{{l}/{r}}}=1
, the root
\textstyle\mathchar 267
and the
fundamental weight vector
\textstyle\mathchar 277
are respectively replaced by a
dilated
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}
and a compressed
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}

satisfying the euclidian properties:

\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}^{2}=\mathrm{k}>2\qquad,\qquad\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{k}}<\frac{1}{2}\qquad,\qquad\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}.\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}=1

(21)

These newly scaled vectors
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}
and
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}
generate 1D sublattices
R̃
k
and
W̃
k
extending the root and the weight lattices
R
2
and
W
2
of SU(2).

Figure 6:
On the left, we represent the square lattice
a\mathbb{Z\times}a\mathbb{Z}
with parameter

It
contains the 1D root lattice
R̃
k
. Here, the 1D

W̃

see the Figure
7

for

An interesting way to realise this structure is obtained using a square
lattice with scaled spacings
\mathtt{a}_{x}=\mathtt{a}_{y}=\sqrt{\mathrm{k/2}}.
In this case, the vectors
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}
and
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}
relate to the canonical simple root and the fundamental weight
vector via
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{k/2}}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}
and

\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{2/k}}\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
and
consequently

\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{k}}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}=\mathrm{k}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}

(22)

In this realisation, the root lattice
R
{}_{\mathrm{k}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}\mathbb{Z}
is generated by
\left\{\mathbf{x}_{n}=n\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}\right\},
depicted in Figure
5

\sqrt{\mathrm{k/2}}\mathbb{Z\times}\sqrt{\mathrm{k/2}}\mathbb{Z}
with

\mathbf{x}_{n}=n\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}=}\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{k}}{\mathrm{2}}}n\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}

Setting
\mathtt{a}_{x}=\mathtt{a}_{y}=\mathrm{a}

we get
\mathbf{k}_{m}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{2}}{\mathrm{k}}}m\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}
that reads also like

\mathbf{k}_{m}=m\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}=\frac{m}{\mathrm{k}}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}\hskip 22.99988pt,\hskip 22.99988pt}m\in\mathbb{Z}

showing that

In conclusion, the two dimensional lattices
(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{2}\mathcal{C}},\Lambda_{\mathrm{2}}^{\ast})

\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}}

=

and
\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{k}}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}^{\prime}

{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}}^{\prime}=0,...,k-1,
we get

\mathbf{w}_{m}=\left(n\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}+n^{\prime}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 267$}}}}}^{\prime}\right)+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}+}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 290$}}}^{\prime}\mathbf{\tilde{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 277$}}}}}^{\prime}

(27)

showing that the dual
\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}}^{\ast}
is a union of

\mathrm{k\times k}
sheets of 2D lattices isomorphic to the real
\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}}
. For odd integers k=2j+1, these sheets form a

j,j
). Moreover, the even self

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}
engendered by the

is composed of k
sheets isomorphic to
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}
. It can also
be expressed like

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}=\{\mathbf{u}_{n,m}=n\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}\mathbf{u}_{1}+\frac{m}{\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}}\mathbf{u}_{2}\}

(28)

The corresponding characteristic matrix
\left(\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}}\right)_{\mathcal{C}}
of the even self dual lattice and the induced metric
\left(g_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}}\right)=\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{T}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 273$}}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}
read as follows

\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}}\end{array}\right)\qquad,\qquad g_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)

(29)

with determinant
\det\Lambda_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}=1.

Finally, we note that the generalisation to higher orders r
>
1,
with central charge values
c_{\text{{l}/{r}}}=\mathrm{r}
,
follows straightforwardly. A detailed study is omitted here.

2.3
From scalars in continuum to lattice QFT

In this subsection, we outline the key steps by which the lattices
introduced earlier will be maneuvered in our study. This construction
provides a formal pathway towards a non perturbative description of
topological matter that we will further develop later on (see
section 5). Focussing on the scalar fields (a fermionic description will be
introduced subsequently via fermionization techniques), the transition from
scalars in continuum to a non perturbative lattice framework can be
summarised as follows:

2.3.1
Case of one real 2D scalar field

X=X({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}},{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}})

We start from the 2D field action
\mathcal{S}\left(X\right)=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{2}}d^{2}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}\mathcal{L}\left(X\right)
in continuum with
quadratic lagrangian density given by
\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}}X\right)\left(\partial^{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 278$}}}}X\right)+\frac{1}{2}m^{2}X^{2}
where we
have added the term
m^{2}X^{2}
as a possible quadratic term. In the
interesting limit
m\rightarrow 0
(required by conformal invariance), the
field equation of the scalar (bosonic string) field reads like (
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}}^{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}^{2}})X=\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}\right)\simeq 0;
it remarkably factorises as
follows

\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 284$}}}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 283$}}}}\right)X\simeq 0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{L}}\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{R}}X\simeq 0\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{R}}\frac{\partial}{\partial{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{L}}X\simeq 0\end{array}\right.

(30)

and admits solutions in terms of left and right moving modes corresponding
to the usual splitting
X=X_{L}+X_{R}
with
X_{L}=X\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{L}\right)

and
X_{R}=X\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{R}\right).
In the euclidian metric, the real
coordinates
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}_{L/R}
get replaced by the complex variables
z,\bar{z}
.

\bullet

Matrix equation
:

By defining
V_{L}\simeq\partial_{L}X
and
V_{R}\simeq\partial_{R}X,

commonly interpreted as the left
J_{L}
and right
J_{R}
chiral currents
but thought of below as just the components of a 2D ”vector” field
\mathcal{V}
in the limit
m\rightarrow 0,
the above equations of motion (
30
) become
\partial_{R}V_{L}\simeq 0
and
\partial_{L}V_{R}\simeq 0
. For finite m, we have
V_{L/R}=(\partial_{L/R}+\frac{m^{2}}{\partial_{R/L}})X
. Setting
\mathcal{V}=(V_{L},V_{R})
, one can recast the dynamics
into a 2
\times
2 matrix equation like
\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}^{L}\partial_{L}+{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}^{R}\partial_{R}\right)\mathcal{V}\simeq 0
with
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}^{L}

and
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 269$}}}^{R}
reading from the following expression

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\partial_{L}\\
\partial_{R}&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}V_{L}\\
V_{R}\end{array}\right)\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\end{array}\right)

(31)

from which we deduce the operators

\mathcal{D}\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\partial_{L}\\
\partial_{R}&0\end{array}\right)\qquad,\qquad\mathcal{D}^{2}\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{L}\partial_{R}&0\\
0&\partial_{R}\partial_{L}\end{array}\right)

(32)

\bullet

Fourier transform
:

In the reciprocal space
\partial_{L/R}=\frac{i}{\hbar}q_{L/R}
and
where the fields
V_{L}
and
V_{R}
are expanded in Fourier series
2
2

2

For exact m=0 (CFT), one uses Laurent expansions and the powerful
properties of complex analysis.
like
\sum_{q}e^{iq.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}\tilde{V}_{L}

and
\sum_{q}e^{iq.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}\tilde{V}_{R}
, the above matrix relation can be
brought to the form

\sum_{\left(q_{L},q_{R}\right)}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&q_{L}\\
q_{R}&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e^{iq.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}\tilde{V}_{L}\\
e^{iq.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}\tilde{V}_{R}\end{array}\right)\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\end{array}\right)

(33)

Notice the emergence of the
Dirac-like
3
3

3

By using ferminoisation ideas, the real bosoinc components
V_{L/R}
can
be expressed in terms of complex fermionic fields as
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 288$}}}_{L/R}{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 288$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 288$}}}_{L/R}^{\ast}.
matrix operators
\mathcal{D}
in (
32
) and the
reciprocal
\mathcal{\tilde{D}}
in (
33
) which generally appear in
fermionic theories. This hints at a possible generalization to a
non-perturbative regime, where the non linear (differential) operator
\boldsymbol{\hat{D}}
involves oscillating (trigonometric) functions like
for instance

\boldsymbol{\hat{D}=}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\sin q_{L}\\
\sin q_{R}&0\end{array}\right)\qquad\rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{\tilde{D}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&q_{L}\\
q_{R}&0\end{array}\right)

(34)

with eigenvalues given by
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 270$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 270$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 270$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 270$}}}_{\pm}\sim\pm\sqrt{\left|\sin q_{L}\sin q_{R}\right|}
which tends to
\pm\sqrt{\left|q_{L}q_{R}\right|}
for small values
q_{L/R}\rightarrow 0.

2.3.2
Lattice extension

In the (euclidian) lattice field description, the continuum fields
V_{L}\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}\right)\equiv V^{+}\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}\right)
and
V_{R}\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}\right)\equiv V^{-}\left({{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}\right)
are discretized in terms of the
two following:
\left(\mathbf{i}\right)
The lattice components
V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}^{+}\equiv V_{\mathbf{n}}^{+}
and
V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}^{-}\equiv V_{\mathbf{n}}^{-}
with discrete coordinates
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}

=n^{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
where
\left(\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}\right)
are generators of the 2D lattice. And
\left(\mathbf{ii}\right)
their
Fourier transforms
given by the
expansions
\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{i\mathbf{k}.\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}\tilde{V}_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}^{+}
and

. Borrowing ideas from tight binding modeling of condensed matter

tm1
-

, the quadratic lattice hamiltonian
describing neighboring interactions read as follows

H=\sum_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}\left(V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}^{+\dagger},V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}^{-\dagger}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}t_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{--}&t_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{-+}\\
t_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{+-}&t_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{++}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{+}\\
V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{-}\end{array}\right)+hc

(35)

where the complex
t_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}}^{\pm\pm}
are coupling functions that can be thought of as constants
for convenience. By using the Fourier transforms while restricting the
interactions to the first nearest neighbours (
\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{m}}=\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}+\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}}}_{j}
with
j=1,...,d
) and
assuming off-diagonal hopping parameters for illustration (eg:
H=t\sum_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}\sum_{j}V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}}^{-\dagger}V_{\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 280$}}}}_{\mathbf{n}}+\mathbf{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}}}_{j}}^{+}+hc
), it reads in
the Fourier space like
H=\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\mathcal{\tilde{V}}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}H_{\mathbf{k}}\mathcal{\tilde{V}}_{\mathbf{k}}
with 2
\times
2
matrix
H_{\mathbf{k}}
given by

H_{\mathbf{k}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&f_{\mathbf{k}}\\
f_{\mathbf{k}}^{\ast}&0\end{array}\right)\qquad,\qquad f_{\mathbf{k}}=t\sum_{j}e^{ik_{j}}

(36)

where we have set
k_{j}=\mathbf{k.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}}}_{j}
and for which the
eigenvalues
E_{\mathbf{k}}^{\pm}
are given by
\pm\sqrt{f_{\mathbf{k}}^{\ast}f_{\mathbf{k}}};
see eq(
123
) for an interesting concrete
model. For the toy example where we only consider the two first closest
neighbors given by
k_{1}=\mathbf{k.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}}}_{1}=k
and
k_{2}=\mathbf{k.{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 274$}}}}_{2}=-k,
we get
f_{\mathbf{k}}=t\cos k
with
t=t_{1}+it_{2}

and consequently

H_{\mathbf{k}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&t\sin\left(\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}}}{2}-k\right)\\
t^{\ast}\sin\left(\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}}}{2}-k\right)&0\end{array}\right)

(37)

with eigenvalues

E_{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{tt^{\ast}\cos^{2}k}\qquad,\qquad-\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}}}{2}\leq k\leq\frac{3{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}}}{2}

(38)

For this toy example, the zero modes of

, which follow from
the solving the condition

, are given by
{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 276$}}}_{\pm}=\pm\frac{{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}}}{2}

\mathop{\mathrm{m}od}\nolimits 2{{}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 281$}}}.
Around these zeros while setting

, the non perturbative Hamiltonian

reduces to the

given by

h_{\mathbf{q}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&tq\\
t^{\ast}q&0\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(q^{3}\right)

which is linear in momentum. The band structure of the tight binding model (

3
Extension to SU(3) Lattices

Construction A of code based NCFTs is deeply connected to the
lattice structures of SU(2) and SO(4) Lie algebras. This
naturally raises the question of whether similar constructions can be
extended to lattices associated with other Lie algebras like for instance
the rank two SU(3), SO(5) and G
2

Y1
-
Y3
. In this section,
we focus on the SU(3) case and apply the algorithm introduced in section 2
to develop a new realisation of NCFTs with central charge
c_{\text{{l}}/\text{{r}}}=2\mathrm{r}
(
\mathrm{r\geq 1}
) thus generalising
the previous (
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{2}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{2}\ast}
). We denote the new lattice triplet like

(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}},\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\ast})

(40)

with the embedding sequence
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\subset\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}\mathcal{C}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\subset\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\ast}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2\mathrm{r},2\mathrm{r}}.
For r=1, the lattices
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\ast}
are 4D, factorising respectively as follows

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}=\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\prime}\qquad,\qquad\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\ast}=\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\times\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\prime}

(41)

where
R
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
and
W
{}_{\mathrm{k}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
are the 2D root and weight lattices of SU(3). By using
the isomorphism

\text{{W}}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}/\text{{R}}_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}

(42)

it results that the discriminant
\Lambda_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}\ast}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{3}}^{\mathbf{su}_{3}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}.
To build the realisation (
40
), we consider the case k=3, supported by the
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{3}}
centre of SU(3) and report the more general cases k
>
3 and k
<
3 to appendix C.
